Overview

This Report contains 44 paragraphs and three Performance Audits relating to
under assessments/non-realisation/short realisation of penalties, taxes, duties etc.
The total money value involved is हैं 268 crore. Some of the major findings are
mentioned below:

> During the year 2010-11, the total revenue raised by the State Government
(₹ 873.14 crore) was 20.47 per cent of the total revenue receipts ( 4,266.02
crore). The balance 79.53 per cent of receipts during 2010-11 comprised of
State’s share of divisible taxes and duties amounting to हैं 901.65 crore and grants-
in-aid amounting to ~ 2491.23 crore. The revenue raised by the State Government
in 2010-11 as compared to 2009-10 was 21.37 per cent higher.

(Paragraph 1.1)

> Test check of the records of taxes on sale, trade etc, state excise, motor
vehicles tax, other tax receipts, forest receipts and other non-tax receipts
conducted during the year 2010-11 revealed underassessment / short / non-levy /
loss of revenue amounting to < 557.24 crore in 240 cases. During the year, the
Departments accepted assessments / short / non levy of revenue of हैं 1695.49
crore in 74 cases pointed out in 2010-11 and earlier years, and recovered हैं 10.73
crore.

(Paragraph 1.5.1)

A Performance Audit of “Cross verification of Declaration form in Inter-
State Trade” and audit of Sales Tax Department revealed the following
irregularities:

We found that the Department had not prepared any Budget Estimates and did not
maintain a database of concessions/exemptions given under CST.
(Paragraph 2.9.6; 2.9.7)

We found that the online filing of application for declaration forms and their issue
has not yet been introduced by the Department; the TINXSYS, site was not being
used for verification purposes by the offices. Besides only the declaration form
“C; were uploaded whereas ‘F’ form were not uploaded, forms were printed in
excess of requirement and the Department was saddled with huge closing stock of
‘C’ and ‘F’ forms which was fraught with the risk of obsolete and damaged
forms.

(Paragraph 2.9.9; 2.9.10)

We found that in four ST field offices 78 dealers submitted 2600 ‘C’ forms after a
delay ranging between 9 months and 40 months in violation of the CST Act. In
STs, Nongpoh and Jowai, two dealers sold coal, iron and steel etc, valued at हें
2.57 crore in course of interstate trade to dealers of Assam and submitted the


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2011-Revenue Receipts

portion marked ‘DUPLICATE’ to the ST instead of the portion marked
‘ORIGINAL’ which was incorrectly accepted by the assessing officers.
(Paragraph 2.9.13; 2.9.14)

We noticed in Shillong and Williamnagar that on reporting loss of four ‘F’ forms
and closure of business by the dealer, the Department did not issue notices for
declaring the forms as invalid/obsolete. We also found that though Taxation
Department of Mizoram had notified 14 forms as obsolete the ST , Nongpoh
Meghalaya accepted these forms incorrectly. The tax due under these forms was
₹ 19.55 lakh.

(Paragraph 2.9.16; 2.9.17)

We found that dealers under ST, Nongpoh Meghalaya sold taxable goods valued at
र₹ 44.19 crore having a tax effect of ~ 1.83 crore during the period 2004-05 to
2008-09 on declaration forms which were not printed/ not issued by the
Commercial Taxes Department of Manipur, Assam and West Bengal States
indicating that the forms were not genuine. Thus evasion of tax cannot be ruled
out in these cases.

(Paragraph 2.9.18)

The declaration forms furnished by three dealers and accepted by the ST,
Nongpoh for sales valued हैं 47.35 lakh were found irregular on cross verification,
since the purchasing dealers in Assam were not found registered for the goods
they had purchased. In absence of cross verification such irregularities are not
being detected by the Department.

(Paragraph 2.9.19.1)

We noticed that ST, Circle I, Shillong accepted 11 ‘F’ forms in support of claim
for exemption of tax and STs, Nongpoh and Jowai accepted 18 ‘C’ forms from
unregistered dealers of other states . The concession availed/exempted on these
forms was हैं 32.22 lakh which remain undetected for want of cross verification by
the Department.

(Paragraph 2.9.19.3)

We found during cross verification that sale of goods valued at हैं 11.11 crore was
made in excess of the purchases depicted against the Declaration Forms. This
escapement of turnover resulted in non levy of tax of ¥ 66.57 lakh.

(Paragraph 2.9.20.1)
We found eleven dealers in five circles concealed turnover of % 1.91 crore and
evaded tax of ¥ 23.88 lakh. Besides, penalty of ¥ 47.76 lakh is also leviable for
wilful concealment of purchases.

(Paragraph 2.9.21.3)

We found that four dealers registered in ST, Nongpoh purchased goods valued at
र₹ 62.38 lakh between 2006-07 and 2009-10 by utilising seven ‘C’ forms.
However our cross-verification revealed that there was variation in nature of
commodity purchased as such concessions allowed were incorrect.

(Paragraph 2.9.21.5)

Overview

We notice that the Sales Tax Department detected 6,000 vehicles in which
transporters carried taxable goods without proper particulars and levied/ collected
composition money of % 0.13 crore instead of ¥ Three crore thereby resulting in
short levy/realisation of composition money of % 2.87 crore.

(Paragraph 2.10.1)

Our cross verification of the records of a dealers with two cement manufacturing
companies revealed that the dealer purchased cement valued at ~ 15.84 crore.
However, dealer was assessed for हैं 86.61 lakh only. Thus the dealer, purchase
turnover of 14.97 crore involving tax of ~ 1.87 crore escaped assessment.

(Paragraph 2.12)

We notice during test check of Williamnagar that the Superintendent Taxes(ST)
had not properly verified the records available with him at the time of making the
assessments, leading to a concealment of turnover of हैं 11.97 crore going
unnoticed thereby resulting in evasion of tax of ¥ 47.87 lakh

(Paragraph 2.14)

We cross-verified the records of three ST circles with three Government
organisations and found that they had not deducted tax at source while purchasing
goods worth % 3.12 crore from five dealers resulting in non levy of tax of = 28
lakh

(Paragraph 2.21)
We noticed that a dealer incorrectly claimed deduction of हैं 44.43 lakh as the
value of boulders, stone and sand used in the execution of works contract. This
deduction was allowed by the ST which was irregular and resulted in under
assessment of tax of ¢ 5.53 lakh. Besides, interest of ~ 3.55 lakh is also leviable.

(Paragraph 2.23)
We noticed during test check of records of ST, Circle I, Shillong that ST
calculated tax at the rate of four per cent instead of 12.5 per cent. Thus, due to
incorrect application of rate, there was short levy of tax of € 17.20 lakh.

(Paragraph 2.28)

We noticed that laxity on the part of the Commissioner of Excise led to allowing
manufacturing and sale IMFL without realisation of licence fee resulted in non-
realisation of licence fee of ¥ 12.30 lakh.

(Paragraph 3.7)

The Performance Audit on ‘Computerisation of Motor Vehicle Department’
revealed the following:

> We noticed that the Transport Department (TD) had not entered into any
annual maintenance contract (AMC) for maintenance and repairing of
hardware. Further, 67 per cent of the servers, 17 per cent of the slim PCs,
55 per cent of the printers, and 75 per cent of UPS with the Department
were not functioning as on 30 August 2011.

(Paragraph 4.7.8.1).

> We noticed that out of the seven DTOs, four DTOs had not completed
computerisation of backlog records of 12,150 registered vehicles. The
backlog of records of 2.07 lakh driving licenses was not cleared in all the
seven DTOs. Thus the State and National Register was incomplete to that
extent.

(Paragraph 4.7.8.2, 4.7.10.4)

> We noticed that there was no fire detection equipment or fire extinguishers
to fight any contingencies, in any of the three DTOs visited and the Office

of the STA, housing the computer for the National Permit System.
(Paragraph 4.7.8.3)

> We noticed that in three DTOs the server were switched on and off on
daily basis, while in one DTO, the server was switched off at varying
intervals and on holidays and weekends. The Department has not laid
down any guidelines laying down the necessity of switching on/off the
servers. We also noticed that log files were deleted without conducting

any analysis leading to loss of audit trail.
(Paragraph 4.7.8.9)

> We noticed that surcharge was not realised from 399 vehicles out of 19719
private vehicles registered in East Khasi Hills Division. The rates of
surcharge could be altered manually as such manual intervention cannot
be ruled out due to this.

(Paragraph 4.7.9.1)

> The data available in the system indicated that three vehicles shared same
registration number and 1,308 vehicles shared 654 registration numbers,
1,277 vehicles shared the same set of engine numbers and 44 vehicles
shared 22 chassis numbers. Out of these, 112 vehicles were registered
after implementation of Vahan.

(Paragraph 4.7.9.3 and 4.7.9.8)

> 190 non-transport (NT) DLs were issued to 55 applicants authorising the
licence holder to drive the same class of vehicle. We further noticed that

1.1.4 _Initiatives for mobilising additional resources

In the State Finance Minister’s Budget speech 2010-117, the following additional
resource mobilisation efforts during 2010-11 were proposed:

(1) Amendment of the Meghalaya Tax on Luxuries (Hotels and Lodging Houses)
Act, 1991 to rationalise the tax on room tariff with a view to encourage and
promote tourism.

(ID) To enhance the rate of VAT on items presently fixed at four per cent to five
per cent.

(गा) Generate additional revenue of around % 10 crore annually from introduction
of holograms in the sale of liquor.

(IV) Additional revenue of % 42 crore expected due to revision of royalty on coal
from % 165 to ₹ 290 per MT.

Our scrutiny however revealed that the Meghalaya Tax on Luxuries (Hotel and
Lodging Houses) Act, 1991 was not amended. Revenues of % 92.73 lakh was
realised on sale of holograms against the estimated ₹ 10 crore. Out of the ₹ 42
crore additional revenue expected due to enhancement of royalty on coal, the
additional revenue on this count over the previous year was only ₹ 17.37 crore
despite increase in coal production from 57.67 lakh MT in 2009-10 to 69.74 lakh
MT during 2010-11.

The wide variation between the expected additional resource mobilisation and
actual collection indicated the need for more analytical, rigorous and realistic
budgetary forecasting methodologies and procedures.

226 March 2010



Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2011-Revenue Receipts

1.2 _ Response of the Government and assurances

1.2.1 Failure of senior officials to enforce accountability and protect the
interest of the State Government

The Principal Accountant General (PAG) (Audit), Meghalaya conducts periodic
inspection of the various offices of the Government Departments to test check the
correctness of assessments, levy and collection of tax and non-tax receipts, and
verify the maintenance of accounts and records as per the Acts, Rules and
procedures prescribed by the Government. These inspections are followed up
with the inspection reports (IRs) issued to the heads of offices inspected with
copies to the higher authorities. Serious irregularities noticed in audit are also
brought to the notice of the Government/head of the Department by the office of
the PAG (Audit). An annual report regarding pending IRs is sent to the
Secretaries of the concerned Government Departments to facilitate monitoring
and settlement of the audit observations raised in these IRs through the
intervention of the Government.

IRs issued upto March 2011 pertaining to the offices under seven Departments*
disclosed that 154 IRs involving money value of % 1,487.85 crore remained
unsettled at the end of June 2011. Of these, 25 IRs containing 83 observations
involving money value of % 172.18 crore pertaining to the offices under six
Departments* had not been settled for more than five years.

In respect of 29 IRs involving money value of % 191.87 crore issued during
2010-11, even the first reply has not been received from the Departments/
Government (September 2011). The status regarding position of old outstanding
IRs/paragraphs was reported to the Government in July 2011; their reply has not
been received (September 2011).

1.2.2__ Departmental Audit Committee Meetings

In order to expedite the settlement of the outstanding audit observations contained
in the IRs, Departmental audit committees have been constituted by the
Government. These committees are chaired by the secretaries of the concerned
administrative Departments and their meetings are attended by the concerned
officers of the State Government and officers of the PAG.

During the year 2010-11, we held three audit committee meetings - two* with
Excise, Registration, Taxation and Stamps Department and one® with Forest
Department in which we discussed 155 IRs containing 474 audit observations. As

* Forest, Mining & Geology, Sales Tax, Stamps & Registration, State Excise, State Lottery and
Transport Departments.

1.2.4__Response of the Departments to the draft audit paragraphs

The draft paragraphs are forwarded to the secretaries of the concerned
Departments through demi-official letters drawing their attention to the audit
findings and requesting them to send their response within six weeks. The fact of
non-receipt of replies from the Departments is invariably indicated at the end of
each such paragraph included in the Audit Report.

44 audit paragraphs and three Performance Audits proposed to be included in the
Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India for the year ended March
2011, Government of Meghalaya were forwarded to the Secretaries of the
respective Departments in July 2011. Out of these, replies were furnished to only
eight paragraphs and two Performance Audits upto October 2011. The remaining
36 paragraphs have been included without the response of the Government.

1.2.5 Follow up on Audit Reports-summarised position

As per Headquarter’s instructions the State PACs are to send paras upto 2007-08
to the concerned Government for follow up. Accordingly, all outstanding paras
for the Audit Reports upto 2007-08 have been referred to the PAC for necessary
action.

1.3 Status of assurances by the Department/Government on the issues
highlighted in the Audit Reports

In order to analyse the system of addressing the issues highlighted in the
Inspection Reports (IRs)/Audit Reports by the Department/Government the action
taken on the paragraphs included in the Inspection Reports/Audit Reports by the
Transport Department is shown in the succeeding paragraphs.



Chapter-I: General

> During the last five years, 32 IRs containing 111 paragraphs involving
money value of % 349.23 crore were issued to the Department/Government.

> Out of the 32 IRs issued during the last five years, even first reply has not
been received in respect of 14 IRs involving money value of = 337.69 crore.

> Out of 111 paragraphs involving money value of &% 349.23 crore, the
Department has accepted paragraphs involving money value of = 10.16 lakh
against which, no recovery has been made in all the cases (September 2011). No
intimation in respect of the remaining has been given to audit (September 2011).

> During 2006-07 to 2010-11, 24 paragraphs and two Performance Audits
involving money value of = 1642.45 crore in respect of Transport Department
have featured in the Audit Reports of the Comptroller and Auditor General of
India, Government of Meghalaya. The Department accepted 7 paragraphs
involving money value of % 1236.43 crore and recovered ₹ 4 lakh. No reply has
been received in respect of the remaining paragraphs.

We recommend that the Government may consider taking suitable steps to install
an effective procedure for prompt and appropriate response to audit observations
as well as taking action against officials/officers who fail to send replies to the
IRs/paragraphs as per the prescribed time schedules and also fail to take action to
recover loss/outstanding demand in a time bound manner.

Government

The Performance Audits conducted by this office are forwarded to the concerned
Departments/Government for their information with a request to furnish their
replies. These Performance Audits are also discussed in exit conferences and the
Departments’/Government’s views are included while finalising the Performance
Audits for the Audit Report.

During the period from 2006-07 to 2010-11, seven Performance Audits pertaining
to Taxation, Forest, Transport, Lottery and Excise Departments containing 65
recommendations were discussed with the Departments/Government. All the
recommendations were accepted with an assurance to look into them. However,
the Departments/Government have taken action in seven recommendation and
following systems have been put in place by the Taxation Department:-

1. The definition of cost price was included in the Meghalaya Excise Rules
(Assam Excise Rules, 1945 as adopted by the Government of Meghalaya).

2. Database of risky dealers. The Department has entrusted the Deputy
Commissioner of Taxes the work relating to framing the parameters for

8

easy detection of dubious/risky dealers. This list is proposed to be
prepared in an electronic format for hoisting in the website.

3. Erection of Integrated checkgate. Action has already been initiated to
establish integrated checkgates and the process of site selection is in
progress.

4. Dealers having turnover over हैं 40 lakh. The Commissioner of Taxes had
requested National Informatics Centre to develop a database of such
dealers.

5. Form for claiming exemption for goods taxable under Schedule V of the
MVAT Act. The Department has prepared E-Return formats for cross
verification of goods taxable under Schedule V of the MVAT Act.

6. Cross verification with Income Tax/Central Excise Department. The
Commissioner has issued instructions for mandatory cross verification of
particulars with Income Tax/Central Excise Departments.

7. The Commissioner has already issued instructions to the STs to furnish
monthly return showing submission of returns/registration of dealers etc.,
to him. Instructions have also been issued for supervisory inspection by
the DCT/ACT.

Though the concerned Departments/Government accepted all the remaining
recommendations, they are yet to streamline the system/amend the provisions as
recommended by us.

We recommend that the Government put in place a monitoring mechanism to
watch and ensure timely action on the recommendations accepted by the
concerned Departments in the best interest of the revenue of the State.

14 Planning for audit during 2010-11

The unit offices under various Departments are categorised into high, medium and
low risk units according to their revenue position, past trends of audit
observations and other parameters. The annual audit plan is prepared on the basis
of risk analysis which inter alia include critical issues in government revenues
and tax administration i.e. budget speech, white paper on state finances, reports of
the finance commission (State and Central), recommendations of the taxation
reforms committee, statistical analysis of the revenue earnings during the past five
years, features of the tax administration, audit coverage and its impact during past
five years etc.

During the year 2010-11, out of 168 auditable units, 96 units were planned and
audited. Besides, three Performance Audits on “Forest receipts”, “Cross
verification of declaration forms in interstate trade or commerce” and
“Computerisation of Motor Vehicles Department” were also conducted.

Chapter-II: Taxes on sale, trade ete.

CHAPTER-IL

TAXES ON SALE, TRADE ETC.

2.71 Tax Administration

Commercial Taxes Department is the most important revenue-earning department
of the State. The Principal Secretary to the Government of Meghalaya, Excise,
Registration, Taxation and Stamps (ERTS) Department, is in overall charge of the
Sales Tax Department at the Government level. The Commissioner of Taxes
(COT) is the administrative head of the Department. He is assisted by two
Deputy Commissioners of Taxes (DCT) and two Assistant Commissioners of
Taxes (ACT). One of the ACT, functions as the appellate authority. At the
district level, the Superintendents of Taxes (ST) have been entrusted with the
work of registration, scrutiny of returns, collection of taxes, levy of interest and
penalty, issue of road permits/declaration forms etc.

The collection of tax, interest and penalty etc., in the State is governed by the
provisions of the Central Sales Tax (CST) Act, 1956, the CST Rules, 1957, the
Meghalaya Value Added Tax (MVAT) Act, 2003 and the MVAT Rules, 2005.
Before the introduction of VAT on 1 May 2005, the Meghalaya Sales Tax (MST)
Act and the Meghalaya Finance (Sales Tax) (MFST) Act were in place, which
have, since been repealed with the introduction of VAT. However, assessments
under the MST Act and MFST Act are still being made. The STs are the
Assessing Officers (AO) under the repealed acts. However, with the introduction
of VAT, an audit team with the DCT as its head has been constituted to assess the
dealers while the STs have been vested with the power to scrutinise returns
furnished by the dealers.

2.7 __ Lack of internal controls in accounting of money receipt books

The internal controls of the Department were weak and even the accounting of
receipt books was not done properly as mentioned in the following paragraphs:-

As per Rule 58 of the Meghalaya Financial Rules, all checkposts are required to
issue receipts in TR Form No 4 while collecting money on behalf of the
Government and maintain stock register of receipt books. The receipt shall be
signed by a duly authorised officer and the amount collected should be entered in
the cash book. The detailed particulars of books received from the issuing
authority and utilised are required to be recorded in a register and authenticated
by the officer-in-charge periodically.

e In Umkiang checkpost, we noticed that particulars of books received,
issued, utilised and balance in stock were not regularly recorded in the
stock register of receipt books. The entries were also not authenticated by
the officer-in-charge of the checkpost periodically. We cross-verified the
particulars of the issue register of receipt books of the COT, Meghalaya
with the stock register of the checkpost and found that 790 receipt books
(100 pages each) were issued to checkpost between 05 March 2008 and 15
March 2010. The checkpost authority however, entered only 780 books in
their stock register. Thus 10 books bearing number 14,881 to 14,890 issued

16


Chapter-II: Taxes on sale, trade ete.

by the COT on 07 December 2009 were not accounted for in the stock
register of the checkpost. This is fraught with the risk of misappropriation.

e The case was reported to the ERTS Department, GOM in September 2010;
reply was awaited (September 2011).

2.9 Performance Audit of Cross verification of Declaration

Forms used in inter-State Trade or commerce

Highlights

A Performance Audit of “Cross verification of Declaration forms in Inter-
State Trade or commerce” and audit of Sales Tax Department revealed the
following irregularities:

> We found that the Department had not prepared any Budget Estimates and
did not maintain a database of concessions/exemptions given under CST.
(Paragraph 2.9.6; 2.9.7)

> We found that the online filing of application for declaration forms and
their issue has not yet been introduced by the Department; the TINXYS site was
not being used for verification purposes by the offices. Besides, only the
declaration form ‘C; were uploaded whereas ‘F’ form were not uploaded and
forms were printed in excess of requirement and the Department was saddled with
huge closing stock of ‘C’ and ‘F’ forms which was fraught with the risk of
obsolete and damaged forms.

(Paragraph 2.9.9; 2.9.10)

> We found that in four ST field offices, 78 dealers submitted 2600 ‘*C’
forms after a delay ranging between 9 months and 40 months in violation of the
CST Act. In STs, Nongpoh and Jowai, two dealers sold coal, iron and steel etc,
valued at % 2.57 crore in course of interstate trade to dealers of Assam and
submitted the portion marked ‘DUPLICATE?’ to the ST instead of the portion
marked ‘ORIGINAL’ which was incorrectly accepted by the assessing officers.

(Paragraph 2.9.13; 2.9.14)

> We noticed in Shillong and Williamnagar that on reporting loss of four ‘F’
forms and closure of business by the dealer, the Department did not issue notices
for declaring the forms as invalid/obsolete. We also found that though Taxation
Department of Mizoram had notified 14 forms as obsolete the ST , Nongpoh
Meghalaya accepted these forms incorrectly. The tax due under these forms was
& 19.55 lakh.

(Paragraph 2.9.16; 2.9.17)

> We found that dealers under ST, Nongpoh Meghalaya sold taxable goods
valued at हैं 44.19 crore having a tax effect of € 1.83 crore during the period 2004-
05 to 2008-09 on declaration forms which were not printed/ not issued by the
commercial taxes Department of Manipur, Assam and West Bengal indicating
that the forms were not genuine. Thus evasion of tax cannot be ruled out.
(Paragraph 2.9.18)

> The declaration forms furnished by three dealers and accepted by the ST,
Nongpoh for sales valued at %47.35 lakh were found irregular on cross

Chapter-II: Taxes on sale, trade etc.

2.9.1 Introduction

Interstate trade is regulated under the Central Sales Tax (CST) Act, 1956, the
Central Sales Tax (Registration & Turnover) (CSTRT) Rules, 1957 and the
Central Sales Tax (Meghalaya) (CSTM) Rules. The tax incentives in form of
concessional rates of tax on goods are granted to dealers under the law in order to
promote trade and commerce in the country.

Under the CST Act, registered dealers are eligible to certain concessions and
exemptions of tax on interstate transactions subject to submission of specified
declaration forms (Forms ‘C’ and ‘F’). It is the responsibility of the State
Taxation Departments to ensure proper accounting of the forms and to take
adequate safeguards against their misutilisation since tax relief allowed on ‘C and
‘F’ forms represents revenue foregone to the State exchequer.

Under the CST Act, every dealer who in the course of interstate trade, sells to a
registered dealer, goods of the classes specified in the certificate of registration of
the purchasing dealer shall be liable to pay tax at the concessional rate of four per
cent upto 31 March 2007, three per cent upto 31 May 2008 and two per cent
thereafter provided such sales are supported by a ‘C’ form. The steps involved in
the process are illustrated below:

= Branch

interstate transactions

Under Section 6A of the CST (Amendment) Act, 1972 transfer of goods not by
reason of sale by a registered dealer to any other place of his business outside the
State or to his agent or principal in other States is exempt from tax on production
of a ‘F’ form duly filled in and signed by the principal officer of the other place of
business or his agent or principal, as the case maybe, along with evidence of
despatch of such goods. The steps involved in the process are as under:

Maintenance of accounts and use of ‘C’ and ‘F’ forms: In Meghalaya ‘C’ and
‘F’ forms are obtained by the COT from the State Government press and supplied
to the STs for issue to registered dealers. The registered dealers issue these forms
to selling dealers/branch/agent/principal in other States while purchasing/
receiving goods for purposes specified in their Registration Certificates (RC) in
order to avail concession/ exemption from payment of tax. The dealers have to
submit utilisation statements for the forms received and utilised by them to the
STs who keeps an account of the same in the assessment records of the dealers.
No fresh forms are to be issued to the dealer until utilisation statements of forms
previously issued to him are submitted.

Tax Information Exchange System (TINXSYS):- TINXSYS is a centralised
exchange of all interstate dealers registered across the various States and Union
Territories, instituted by the Empowered Committee (EC) of State Finance
Ministers as a repository of interstate transactions taking place among various
States and Union Territories (UT). The website was designed to help the Taxation
Departments of States and UT to effectively monitor interstate trade. It can also
be used by any dealer to verify the counter party interstate dealer in any other
State and by Taxation Department officials to verify ‘C’ and ‘F’ forms issued by
other State Taxation Departments and submitted to them by the dealers in support
of claim for concessions/exemptions. TINXSYS additionally provides
Management Information System and Business Intelligence Reports to the
Taxation Departments to monitor interstate trade movement and enables the EC to
monitor the trends in interstate trade.

Receipt and issue of declaration Forms: - The receipt and issue of ‘C’ and ‘F’
forms are accounted for in stock registers of the ST field offices. When the forms
are issued by the ST to the dealer, the latter’s signature, as token of receipt, is to
be obtained in the register. A registered dealer to whom ‘C’ and ‘F’ forms are

22


Chapter-II: Taxes on sale, trade ete.

issued is required to maintain an account of such forms and to furnish an
utilisation statement of the forms to the ST in the prescribed format.

Penalty for misuse of ‘C’ and ‘F’ forms:- Under Section 10 of the CST Act, if
any person being a registered dealer, falsely represents when purchasing any class
of goods that goods of such class are covered by his RC or not being a registered
dealer, falsely represents when purchasing goods in the course of interstate trade
that he is a registered dealer or after purchasing any goods for any of the specified
purposes make use of the goods for any such purposes the assessing authority
may impose upon him by way of penalty a sum not exceeding one and a-half
times the tax which would have been levied under the CST Act. The penalty may
be imposed and collected in the State in which the purchasing dealer is/should
have been registered.

2.9.2 _ Organisational setup

The Commissioner of Taxes (COT), Government of Meghalaya is responsible for
administration of the Act and Rules including printing, receipt and distribution of
‘C’ and ‘F’ forms amongst the Superintendent of Taxes (ST) field office. At
headquarters, he is assisted by one Deputy Commissioner of Taxes (DCT), two
Assistant Commissioners of Taxes (ACT) and two Superintendents of Taxes
(STs) in the Enforcement Branch (EB). In addition, the COT has 14! ST field
offices. The STs of the field offices directly receive the ‘C’ and ‘F’ forms from
the COT. They are responsible for accounting of the forms received including
registration and assessment of dealers and issue of forms to them. The STs of the
EB are responsible for interstate investigation/cross-verification of transactions
with other States.

The Principal Secretary to the Government of Meghalaya, Excise, Registration,
Taxation and Stamps (ERTS) Department, is in overall charge of the Sales Tax
Department at the Government level.

2.9.3 Audit objectives

The Performance Audit aims to ascertain whether:

> There exists a fool proof system for custody and issue of the ‘C’ and ‘F’
forms.
> Exemptions/concessions of tax granted by the STs were supported by the

original ‘C’ and ‘F’ forms.
> There is a system for ascertaining genuineness of the forms by cross
verification so as to prevent evasion of tax.

> There is a system in place for uploading information in the TINXSYS
website and the data available therein is utilised for verifying the correctness of
the forms.

1 STs, Shillong (Circle I to Circle VIII), Nongpoh, Jowai, Khlichriat, Tura, Williamnagar,

Nongstoin.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2011- Revenue Receipts

> Appropriate steps are taken on receipt and detection of fake, invalid and
defective” forms.
> The internal control system is adequate and effective so as to prevent

leakage of revenue.

2.9.5 Acknowledgement

The Indian Audit and Accounts Department acknowledges the co-operation of the
Excise, Registration, Taxation and Stamps (ERTS) Department in providing the
necessary information and records to audit. We held an entry conference in
November 2010 in which the objectives, scope and methodology of audit were
explained. The conference was attended by the Deputy Secretary to the
Government of Meghalaya, Finance Department, the COT and the DCT. The
review was forwarded to the Department and to the Government in August 2011.
The findings of the review were discussed in the exit conference held in
November 2011. The replies received during the exit conference and at other
point of time have been appropriately incorporated in the relevant paragraphs.

System deficiencies

2.9.6 Absence of a separate Budget Estimate for receipts under CST Act

We noticed that no separate budget estimates are prepared for collection of
revenue under the CST Act. As a result of which, the Department had no
mechanism to forecast/estimate CST revenue collection. This was not conducive
to sound financial management and was not in consonance with sound budgeting
practices. A table showing the year-wise collection under CST is given below:

Year CsT Tax Total Percentage of CST revenue as against

It will be seen from the above that CST forms a sizeable amount of the
Government revenue. This is mainly due to the fact that the State is a major

exporter of minerals mainly coal and lime stone. As can be seen from the above,
the revenue collection under the CST Act has shown steep falls and rises during
the last four years. The reasons despite being called for (September 2011) were
not furnished.

We recommend that the Government may consider issuing directions to the
Department for framing budget estimates in respect of revenue realisable from
CST and watch collections against the budget estimates.

2.9.7 Absence of database of exemptions/concessions availed

Under the CST Act, concessions and exemptions are allowed to the dealer on sale
of goods in course of interstate trade for which the selling dealer has to furnish
“(7 and ‘F’ forms as the case may be.

25



Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2011- Revenue Receipts

We noticed that the Department, both at the Directorate level as well as at the
field offices level did not maintain a database of concessions/exemptions given
under CST. Such a database is essential so that the Department can monitor the
abuse or misuse, if any, of the concessions/exemptions allowed. As a result, the
Department was not in a position to quantify the amount of revenue foregone due
to concessions/exemptions nor could it monitor or detect the abuse/ misuse of *C’
and ‘F’ forms which have been brought out in the ensuing paragraphs.

We recommend that a centralised database maybe created so that the Department
is in a position to quantify the amount of revenue foregone due to
concessions/exemptions and monitor the abuse or misuse of the provision relating
to concessions/exemptions.

2.9.8 _Non-issue of notification for defective declaration forms

CST Rules provide that the Commissioner may, by notification, declare forms of
particular series as obsolete and invalid with effect from such date as may be
specified in the notification. Out of the nine selected ST field offices, we noticed
in two’ ST field offices that 111° forms were damaged and not fit for use. The
STs did not bring this to the notice of COT for issue of notification. We
apprehend that the forms could be misused by the dealers in purchase of goods.

2.9.9 Non-utilisation of Tax Information Exchange System for
uploading of declaration forms

TINXSYS inter-alia provides Management Information System and Business
Intelligence Reports to the Taxation Departments to monitor interstate trade
movement and enables the EC to monitor the trends in interstate trade.

We found that the receipt and issue of ‘C’ and ‘F’ forms were accounted for in
stock registers of the ST field offices manually and online filing of application for
declaration forms and their issue has not yet been introduced by the Department.
Thus an essential feature of the system which could serve as a monitoring control
for watching the declaration forms remained un-utilised.

2.9.9.1 Partial uploading of data in TINXSYS

The Finance Ministry, GOI, had instituted the TINXSYS website and asked the
States to make it operational and usable for uploading data relating to ‘C’ and ‘F’
forms issued and for verification of forms on an All India Basis.

We noticed that the Department had uploaded only ‘C’ Forms in the TINXSYS
website and no ‘F’ Forms were uploaded in the system. The position of ‘C’ and
‘F’ forms issued and uploaded (furnished by the COT and ST field offices) was as
follows:

of the system which could serve as a monitoring control for watching the
declaration forms remained unutilised.

We recommend that the TINXSYS website be updated for all declaration forms
issued under the Act and be utilised by the Department to improve efficiency,
efficacy and transparency.

2.9.13 Non-compliance of instructions relating to issue of ‘C’ and ‘F’
forms

As per the directions issued by the COT on 02 December 1996 to all the STs, new
declaration forms should be issued only after the utilisation statement in the
prescribed format was submitted to the ST by the indenting dealers.

Test check of records of the nine” selected ST field offices revealed that the
utilisation statements were not submitted by the registered dealers of Tura and
Jowai and were partially furnished by the dealers of Shillong and Nongpoh. We
did not find any record or instance where the STs insisted on submission of the
same before issue of fresh forms. Issue of the declaration forms without
submission of utilisation statement was incorrect and indicated that the STs were
not following the departmental instructions to prevent evasion of tax, concealment
of turnover by misutilisation of forms, etc. This also indicated lack of monitoring
by the COT to ensure compliance of its instruction.

ZI ESE Incorrect acceptance of belatedly submitted declaration forms

As per Rule 12(1) of the CSTRT Rules, 2005 (as amended on 16 September
2005) ‘C’ or ‘F’ forms shall be furnished to the prescribed authority within three
months after the end of the period to which the transaction relates. The prescribed
authority may, however, allow the extension of time limit if the dealer was
prevented by sufficient cause from furnishing the forms.

We noticed during test check of ‘C’ forms that in four'® ST field offices, 78
dealers sold goods valued at हैं 291.04 crore between 2006-07 and 2009-10 and
submitted 2600 ‘C’ forms belatedly. The period of delay in submission of forms
ranged between 9 months and 40 months. We found that in most of the cases the
delay arose as the dealers submitted the forms only at the time of finalising the

In the above cases, the STs accepted the ‘C’ and ‘F’ forms beyond the prescribed

in loss of revenue particularly in case of closure of businesses by the dealers.

It is recommended that the Department may in the interest of revenue issue

that exemption/concession of tax is not be allowed on the forms submitted after
the prescribed period of three months without any recorded reasons.

etc, valued at हैं 2.57 crore in course of interstate trade to dealers of Assam and
furnished seven ‘C’ forms. The STs assessed the dealers at concessional rate. Our
scrutiny however, revealed that the dealers submitted the portion of the form
marked ‘DUPLICATE?’ to the ST instead of the portion marked ‘ORIGINAL’.
Such irregular acceptance of ‘DUPLICATE’ portion of the form was not correct

and verification of original ‘C’ form needs to be done before the concessional rate

2.9.15 Incorrect acceptance of incomplete ‘C’ and ‘F’ forms

Under Section 8(4) (1) (a) read with Section 8(1) of the CST Act, the selling
dealer shall not be entitled to avail tax at concessional rate unless he furnishes
“C’/‘F’ form duly filled and signed by the purchasing dealer/agent containing the
prescribed’ particulars.

Our scrutiny revealed that though there were a number of discrepancies in the
declaration forms, but the assessing authorities incorrectly allowed concessional
rate/exemption from tax.

Analysis of 850 ‘C’ forms (25 per cent) out of 3,537 ‘C’ forms'*, randomly
selected revealed that the columns indicating ‘date from which registration is
valid’ , ‘purchase order no. and date’, ‘challan no. and date’ and ‘name of goods’
were not filled up. In 22 ‘C’ forms ‘seal of the issuing authority’ was not affixed
while in 35 ‘C’ forms ‘RC no. of the purchasing dealer’ was not mentioned.

Similarly we randomly selected 121 ‘F’ forms out of 242 ‘F’ forms (50 per cent)
and found that in none of the selected 121 forms were the ‘date of issue’, ‘date
from which registration is valid’, ‘date on which delivery was taken by the
transferee’ and ‘RC no. of transferee’ filled up.

Though the above forms were incomplete and were thus liable to be rejected at
the time of assessment, the STs accepted all these incomplete forms and granted
exemption/concession to dealers on the goods valued at हैं 777.50 crore. Thus
concession/exemption from payment of tax of ¥ 53.96 crore was allowed without
any verification.

The fact that improperly filled up forms were unreservedly accepted by the STs,
indicates prevalence of slackness in the Department. This extended an open
invitation to dealers to abuse/misuse ‘C’/’F’ forms and unscrupulously avail
concessions/exemptions.

2.9.16 Non-issue of notification in respect of lost and unused
declaration forms

2. 9.16.1 Under the CSTM Rules, if a form received by the dealer is
reported lost, destroyed or stolen, the dealer shall report the fact to the ST and the
COT shall issue public notice of the loss, destruction or theft.

In ST, Circle III, Shillong we noticed that a dealer reported loss of four ‘F’ forms
in July 2009 to the ST. But we found that the matter was not reported to COT by
the ST. Similarly in Jowai, one ‘C’ form was reported lost. The ST reported the
matter to the COT but the COT did not take action. Consequently, notices for
declaring the forms as invalid/obsolete were not issued by the Department

9.16. Under the CSTM Rules, any unused form remaining in stock with
a dealer on the date of cancellation of his RC shall be surrendered to the ST
within 15 days of such cancellation. If the dealer fails to surrender unused forms
at the time of cancellation of his RC, the COT shall issue notification in the
official gazette (including the official gazettes of all other States) to make the
form obsolete and invalid to prevent misuse of such forms.

In ST, Williamnagar, RC of a dealer was cancelled (December 2009) but the
dealer did not surrender three unused forms. The ST neither asked the dealer to
return the forms nor did it report the matter to the COT for making the forms
invalid and obsolete so as to prevent their misuse.

In all the above cases, the misuse of the said forms cannot be ruled out.

2,977 Loss of revenue due to acceptance of obsolete forms

To prevent evasion of tax through use of obsolete/invalid declaration forms by
dealers of other States, the COT, shall, on receipt of the copy of the notification
issued by the other States declaring such forms as obsolete/invalid, forward the
notification to all the concerned STs to enable them to cross-check the forms
submitted by dealers during assessments.

We noticed that two!® dealers in ST, Nongpoh and one" in ST, Jowai sold taxable
goods valued at ~ 2.02 crore for the period from 1998-99 to 2006-07 in course of
inter-state trade to Mizoram and furnished 14 ‘C’ forms. The STs accepted
between 2007-08 and 2008-09, the forms and assessed the dealers at concessional
rate accordingly.

Cross-verification by us with the Taxation Department of Mizoram revealed that
all the forms had been declared obsolete by the COT, Mizoram and necessary
notification in this respect was issued on 13 October 1999 and 29 January 2003,
copies of which were sent to all the COTs of other States. The COT, Meghalaya
however, did not forward the notification to his STs. As such, the STs could not

Chapter-II: Taxes on sale, trade ete.

detect the obsolete and invalid forms at the time of assessment resulting in under
assessment of tax of € 19.55 lakh'®.

2.9.18 Acceptance of the declaration forms not issued by the
commercial tax authorities

To prevent evasion of tax through fake forms, we found that the Department had
not installed a system to cross-verify the forms furnished by the purchasing
dealers/agents of other States. Absence of this system resulted in grant of
concessional rate of tax on invalid obsolete forms.

9.18.1] We noticed that three dealers! in ST, Nongpoh (five forms) and two”?
dealers in ST, Jowai (two forms) sold taxable goods! valued at हैं 2.52 crore
during 2004-05 to 2008-09 in course of interstate trade to dealers of Manipur and
Assam and furnished ‘C’ forms to claim concessional rate of tax. The STs
accepted (2008-09) the forms and assessed the dealers accordingly. However
cross-verification of ‘C’ forms with the commercial taxes Departments of the
concerned States revealed that these declaration forms were not printed at all by
them and they had not issued these forms. As such, it was clear that the forms
were not genuine.

The assessing authorities, neither at the time of, nor at any time before finalising
the assessments, had made any inquiry to ascertain that the particulars contained
in the declarations furnished by the dealers are true Thus, the possibility of
utilisation of fake forms, by the dealers for evasion of tax of = 21.96 [ता सात
be ruled out.

We noticed in STs, Jowai and Tura that three dealers” despatched coal
valued at हैं 31.75 crore by way of stock transfer to dealers of West Bengal and
furnished 11 ‘F’ forms to claim exemption from payment of tax. The STs
accepted between 2007-08 and 2008-09 the forms and exempted the dealers
accordingly. Cross-verification by us with the Taxation Department of West
Bengal (WB), however, revealed that the said ‘F’ forms were issued to other
dealers in WB. Thus, forms submitted in support of the claim by the dealers in

crore during 2004-05 to 2009-10 to dealers of Assam and West Bengal and
furnished 28 ‘C’ forms in support of their claim. The STs accepted the forms and
assessed the dealers at concessional rate. On cross-verification of the forms with
the Taxation Departments of the two States, we noticed that these were not issued
to the purchasing dealers of the respective States. Acceptance of these forms in
support of the claim by STs without any cross verification was irregular and led to
evasion of tax of & 34.09 lakh”.

2.9.19 Accepting forms from unregistered dealers of other States

Under Section 7 of the CST Act, no dealer shall carry on business unless he is
registered and possesses a certificate of registration. Further, under section
8(4)(C) of the Act, a registered dealer shall obtain ‘C’ and ‘F’ forms from the
prescribed authority where he is registered. Under Section 10A of the CST Act, if
a dealer furnishes a false declaration in support of his purchase, he is liable to pay
penalty by way of composition of offence, a sum not exceeding one and half
times the amount of tax.

2.9.19.1 Incorrect acceptance of incomplete ‘C’ forms from dealers not
registered for the goods for which forms were issued

Three dealers in ST, Nongpoh sold goods valued at % 47.35 lakh in course of
interstate trade to two dealers in Assam and furnished declaration forms in
support of their claims. Our cross-verification with the Taxation Department of
Assam revealed that the dealers of Assam were not registered for the goods they
had purchased. This could not be detected as nature of goods sold was not
mentioned in the ‘C’ forms produced in support of their claim. This is evident
from the following table:

2.9.19.2 Incorrect acceptance of ‘C’ forms received from dealers of
other States who had closed down their businesses

Three dealers”® of Meghalaya sold taxable goods valued at हैं 8.17 crore to dealers
of Jammu and Kashmir, Mizoram and Arunachal Pradesh during 2005-06 to
2009-10 and produced five ‘C’ forms. The ST accepted the forms and assessed
the dealers at concessional rate. Cross-verification of the forms however, revealed
that the purchasing dealers of other States had already closed down their business
and the RCs of the dealers were cancelled at the time of purchase of goods but
those dealers had not surrendered the forms at the time of cancellation. Thus,
submission of the invalid forms led to a tax evasion of हैं 20.22 lakh”” which
remained undetected by the Department at the time of finalising the assessments
due to absence of system of cross verification of the declaration forms.

2.9.19.3 Incorrect exemption/concessions availed of on forms from
unregistered dealers of other States

We noticed in ST, Circle I, Shillong that a dealer”* in Meghalaya made stock
transfers of steel wool valued at हैं 2.39 crore to his agent in Mizoram for the
period from 2005-05 to 2006-07 and furnished 11 ‘F’ forms in support of claim
for exemption of tax. The ST accepted the forms as valid and assessed the dealer
accordingly. On cross-verification, we noticed that the dealer in Mizoram was
neither registered nor were the ‘F’ forms issued to him. As such, the acceptance
of the ‘F’ forms by the Meghalaya registered dealer was not correct and the dealer
was liable to pay tax of = 29.88 lakh”.

Similarly we noticed that three*’ dealers in ST, Nongpoh and one”! in ST, Jowai
sold taxable goods to dealers of Mizoram and Arunachal Pradesh valued at € 3.70
crore during 2006-07 to 2008-09 and furnished 18 ‘C’ forms. The STs accepted
the forms as valid and assessed the dealers at concessional rate. Cross-verification
of forms, however, revealed that the forms were not issued to the dealers and they
were not registered with the commercial tax Departments of those States. Thus,
the unregistered dealers procured forms and furnished the same to the registered
dealers of Meghalaya who on the basis of these forms availed tax concessions
amounting to हैं 32.22 lakh”. This irregularity escaped the notice of the Assessing
Authorities due to the absence of system of cross verifications.

2.9.20 Furnishing of incorrect sales turnover

Determination of actual turnover is an essential process for correctly levying the
tax. Sales not supported by declaration forms are liable to higher rate of tax.

2.9.20.1 Inflation of sales turnover by selling dealers of the State

We found that nineteen dealers registered in STs, Nongpoh, Jowai and Shillong
sold taxable goods (timber, electrical goods etc;) valued at हैं 20.78 crore during
2004-05 to 2008-09 in course of interstate trade to dealers of Mizoram, Manipur,
Assam, Gujarat and furnished 60 ‘C’ forms. The STs accepted (March 2007 and
March 2010) the forms without any verification either through TINXSYS or
through correspondence. We found during cross verification of the records of the
respective Commercial Taxes Departments of the concerned States that the
dealers of these states had shown inter-state purchase of goods valued at हैं 9.67
crore only in their accounts against these forms. Thus, sale of goods valued at
र₹ 11.11 crore was made in excess of the purchase shown against the declaration
forms. The escaping of excess turnover of = 11.11 crore with tax effect of = 66.57
lakh* needs investigation. Further, in case of default, penalty of = 1.33 crore
was also leviable.

2.9.20.2 Suppression of sales turnover by selling dealers of the State

We also found that four dealers registered in ST, Nongpoh sold goods (timber,
electrical goods, soft drinks etc) valued at हैं 2.41 crore to dealers of Assam and
furnished five ‘C’ forms. The turnover disclosed in the forms was accepted by the
ST and the dealers were assessed accordingly. Our cross-verification of the ‘C’
forms with books of accounts of the purchasing dealers of other States revealed
that the dealers had shown interstate purchase of goods valued at हैं 2.85 crore
against these forms. Thus, the selling dealers supressed sales turnover of
% 44.31 lakh having a tax effect of € 5.54 lakh®>.

2.9.21 Discrepancies noticed in the transactions of the purchasing
dealers of the State

During the course of all-India cross-verification of ‘C’ and ‘F’ forms, we also
verified 156 forms pertaining to purchasing dealers registered with the nine
selected ST field offices of the state. These forms were issued by the dealers of
other States/UTs and were verified by us. The results are set out in the following
paragraphs.

Chapter-II: Taxes on sale, trade ete.

2921 Incorrect grant of concessional rate of tax on Goods not covered
in the RC of the dealers

Under Section 7(4) of the CST Act, RC can be amended when the registered
dealer files application for inclusion of additional items in the RC to the
registering authority. As per Section 10(C) of the Act ibid, if any registered dealer
falsely represents when purchasing any class of goods that goods of such class are
covered by his RC, he is liable to pay penalty of a sum not exceeding one and half
times the tax which would have been levied under Section 8(2) of the Act. Every
registered dealer is required to submit utilisation certificates in respect of the ‘C’
forms supplied to them showing the value and the kind of goods purchased by
them.

We noticed that 21 dealers in STs, Shillong, Jowai, Nongpoh and Tura, imported
taxable goods (motor vehicles, building materials etc.) valued at ~ 7.47 crore
during 2005-06 to 2009-10 by utilising 194 ‘C’ forms. On cross-verification of
the utilisation statements of the ‘C’ forms furnished by the dealers, we noticed
that the goods purchased were not included in the RCs of the purchasing dealers
of the State. The concessional rate of tax was not applicable in these cases. This
resulted in less realisation of tax = 52.07 lakh*®, Besides, penalty not exceeding
₹ 78.11 lakh*’ was also leviable. The misutilisation of ‘C’ forms by the dealers
was made possible since furnishing of utilisation statements was not insisted upon
by the STs.

2.9.21.2 Evasion of tax by misutilisation of declaration forms in
purchasing cement

We found during test check of records of the ST, Circle-I, Shillong that a dealer
४87३४ was registered for sale and purchase of vegetables, fruits, onion, tea leaves,
pulses, edible oil, under both the MVAT Act and CST Act. He was not registered
for sale and purchase of cement taxable at 12.5 per cent.

Our cross-verification of ‘C’ form issued by the dealer ‘A’ with the records of
another dealer (87 registered in Tezpur Sales Tax Unit office, Assam revealed
that the dealer ‘A’ had purchased cement valued at हैं 18.96 lakh from ‘B’ between
January 2007 and March 2008 by utilising two declarations in form ‘C’. However,
this sale was not disclosed by the dealer and consequently no tax was paid. This
concealment of purchase of = 18.96 lakh resulted in evasion of tax of = 2.37 lakh.
Besides, he was also liable to pay interest of € 1.58 lakh and penalty of हैं 4.74
lakh for wilful concealment of such purchase.

29213: Wilful concealment of purchase turnover

We noticed that two dealers in ST, Circle-II, Shillong, one dealer in ST, Circle-
VI, Shillong, one dealer in ST, Jowai and seven dealers in ST, Nongpoh
purchased taxable goods by utilising ‘C’ forms for हैं 2.29 crore in course of
interstate trade during 2005-06 to 2009-10 at concessional rates. The dealers,
however, disclosed purchase of % 37.67 lakh in their accounts as well as
utilisation statements of ‘C’ forms. Thus, the dealers concealed purchase of € 1.91
crore and evaded tax of = 23.88 lakh”. Besides, penalty of हैं 47.76 lakh"! is also
leviable for wilful concealment of purchase.

2.9.21.4 Underassessment of tax due to acceptance of invalid ‘C’ forms

Rule 12(1) of the CST Rules, 1957 provides that a single declaration may cover
all transactions of a sale which take place in a quarter of a financial year. In case
the delivery of goods is spread over to different quarters in a financial year,
separate declaration forms shall be furnished in respect of goods delivered in each
quarter of a financial year.

We noticed during test check of records of the ST, Circle VIII that a dealer”
disclosed sale of raw limestone valued at हैं 1.71 crore in course of interstate trade
to registered dealers between April 2006 and March 2008. The dealer submitted
four declarations in form ‘C’ and claimed payment of tax at concessional rate. We
however, noticed that all the ‘C’ forms covered transactions of more than one
quarter in a financial year. As such, the forms should have been declared invalid.
Thus, acceptance of invalid forms led to under assessment of tax of € 7.60 lakh.
Besides, interest of ¥ 7.33 lakh was also leviable for default in payment of tax.

utilising seven ‘C’ forms. Cross-verification of the forms however, revealed that
there was variation in nature of commodity purchased as detailed below:

2.9.22 Grant of exemption on sale was not supported by declaration
Sforms/documentary evidence

As per the Industries (Sales Tax Exemption) Scheme, 2001 read with Section 8(5)
of the CST Act, interstate sales are exempt from payment of tax subject to
production of necessary declaration forms/documentary evidence in support of
such sales.

We noticed in ST, Nongpoh that 14 industrial units sold goods valued at
% 120.95 crore in course of interstate trade. However, there was nothing on record
to show that interstate sales were supported by any declaration
forms/documentary evidence. As such, the correctness of exemption of % 10.91
crore allowed by STs while finalising assessments (April 2007 and April 2010)
could not be verified by audit.

Similarly, in ST, Williamnagar we noticed in January 2011 that two dealers” sold
coal valued at = 15.99 crore in course of interstate trade between January 2008
and September 2009 and claimed payment of tax at concessional rate. The ST
assessed the dealers accordingly between April 2008 and December 2009.
However, the forms were not found on record. As such, their correctness could
not be ascertained.

We reported the cases to the ERTS Department, GOM in March 2011; reply was
awaited (June 2011).

2.9.23 Incorrect application of rate

Under Section 8 of the CST Act, tax in respect of interstate sale of goods covered
under form ‘C’ is leviable at three per cent upto 31 May 2008 and two per cent
thereafter.

We noticed during scrutiny of the records of ST, Williamnagar that two” dealers
sold coal valued at हैं 3.33 crore between April and May 2008 in course of
interstate trade and furnished declarations in form ‘C’ in support of their sales.

+ (i) M/s Sanam Traders (ii) M/s S.R. Marak Coal Agency.
4 (3) M/s Nangwal Coal Agency (ii) M/s Himalaya Coal Agency

39



Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2011- Revenue Receipts

The ST while assessing the dealers in July 2008 however erroneously calculated
the tax to be paid by them at the rate of two per cent instead of three per cent due
to which tax of € 3.33 lakh was under assessed.

2.9.24 Conclusion

The review revealed a number of system deficiencies regarding receipt, issue and
utilisation of “C’ and ‘F’ forms. These include absence of a reliable database of
concessions, exemptions and the revenue foregone, absence of a system for
ascertaining the genuineness and correctness of declaration forms submitted by
the dealers for claiming concessions and exemption of tax. The functioning of the
Enforcement Branch left much to be desired. During the period under review, we
observed that the Branch did not find even a single case of utilisation of
fake/irregular forms. Non-compliance of the existing Acts, Rules and
administrative instructions led to leakage of considerable amount of revenue. ‘C’
and ‘F’ forms submitted by the dealers were not uploaded 100 per cent in the
TINXSYS thereby hampering the process of cross-verification and facilitating tax
evasion. The Department needs to strengthen its system of monitoring the
declaration forms.

2.9.25 Summary of Recommendation

We recommend that Government may consider:

> prescribing a system for maintaining a database of concessions and
exemptions of tax and the revenue foregone due to use of ‘C’ and ‘F’ forms.

> prescribing a system for ascertaining the genuineness of the forms
submitted by the dealers through cross-verification of transaction with the issuing
States.

> issuing directions to the Department for framing budget estimates in
respect of revenue realisable from CST and watch collections against the budget
estimates.

> inserting a provision in the Rules to provide for annual physical
verification of forms by COT/STs and destruction of damaged forms on orders of
competent authority.

> revising the rate of sale price of ‘C’ and ‘F’ forms, as the rate fixed by the
Department has not been revised since the last two decades; besides, a uniform
sale price may be fixed for the entire State.

> issuing necessary instructions for timely submission of the declaration
forms and ensuring that exemption/concession of tax is not allowed on the forms
submitted after the prescribed period of three months without any recorded
reasons and

> TINXSYS website be made operational urgently and all assessing officers
be advised to use it for cross-verification of transactions.

Umkiang ST checkpost forwarded these details to the ST, Williamnagar in April
2010, the ST did not take note of this information while assessing the two dealers
to tax in May 2010. Thus, the lapse on the part of the ST to properly verify all
connected records available with him at the time of making the assessments led to
a concealment of turnover of % 11.97 crore going unnoticed thereby resulting in
evasion of tax of = 47.87 lakh. Besides, interest of हैं 8.62 lakh and penalty of
% 95.74 lakh was also leviable.

The case was reported to the ERTS Department, GOM in March 2011; reply was
awaited (September 2011).

of the Act.

2.23 Irregular grant of exemption

—
Boulder, stone and sand are taxable goods
and therefore their value forms part of the
contractual transfer price when these
materials are used in the execution of works
contract** and cannot be deducted from the
turnover.

** Cooch Behar Contractors Association and others
Vs State of West Bengal and others (1996) 103 STC
477 (SC)

We noticed during test check
of the assessment records of
the ST, Circle I, Shillong in
February 2010 that a dealer*®
executed works contract
valued at % 2.93 crore
between July 2006 and
March 2008 and claimed
deduction of हैं 0.96 crore
towards labour, power, fuel
and water charges. Besides, the dealer also claimed deduction of हैं 44.43 lakh as
the value of boulders, stone and sand used in the execution of works contract from
the gross turnover during the aforesaid period which was duly accepted by the ST.
Since boulders, stone and sand were used in the execution of works contract, their
value forms part of the contractual price and therefore cannot be deducted from
the gross turnover. Thus the grant of exemption by the ST was irregular and
resulted in under assessment of tax of € 5.53 lakh. Besides, interest of = 3.55 lakh
is also leviable.

We reported the case to the ERTS Department, GOM in April 2010; reply was
awaited (September 2011).

furnish evidence of export of goods along with ‘H’ forms which is mandatory as

not correct as it is mandatory on the part of the exporting dealer to collect copies
of all export documents from the purchasing dealer and furnish the same to the ST
along with ‘H’ forms to claim exemption from payment of tax in respect of export
within the meaning of section 5(3) of the CST Act.

2.25 Irregular exemption on scrap

We noticed
Under the Meghalaya Industries (Sales Tax Exemption) \ during scrutiny
Scheme, 2001 eligible industrial units are entitled to tax | of the
exemption on sale of finished goods produced. For claiming assessment
exemption, the dealer has to furnish an Eligibility Certificate | records of the
(EC) from the Industries Department and a Certificate of | ST, Nongpoh
Authorisation (CA) from the ERTS Department wherein | in August 2010
items to be manufactured are recorded. that a
manufacturer”
was exempted from payment of tax under the Meghalaya Industries (Sales Tax
Exemption) Scheme, 2001 for manufacture of GI pipes, poles etc. We noticed
from the audited annual accounts of the unit that the dealer sold finished goods
valued at हैं 94.72 crore and scrap valued at हैं 2.04 crore during April 2004 and
March 2006. The dealer claimed tax exemption not only on the sale of finished
goods but on the sale of scrap as well. The ST assessed the dealer accordingly.
Since ‘scrap’ is not a ‘finished good’ under the Meghalaya Industries (Sales Tax
Exemption) Scheme, 2001 the grant of tax exemption on this count by the ST was
irregular and resulted in under assessment of tax of € 8.16 lakh.

The ST in March 2011 did not accept our observation and stated that the
exemption granted was in order as scrap was derived as a result of manufacturing
process and as such the unit was a manufacturer of scrap also. We are of the view
that the ST’s contention is not in keeping with the spirit and objective of the
Meghalaya Industries (Sales Tax Exemption) Scheme, 2001 which is to provide
tax relief to industrial units in the State producing finished goods. Against this
background, it would be utterly erroneous to interpret that any discarded/waste
material arising out of the manufacturing process (‘scrap’ in this case) is also a
finished good eligible for tax relief.

© M/s Nezone Industries Ltd.

Recommendation: In order to bring clarity, uniformity of application/
interpretation to this issue State-wide, it is recommended that the ERTS
Department issue a clarification on this matter to all STs of the Department.

We reported the case to the ERTS Department, GOM in September 2010; reply
was awaited (September 2011).

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

Increase in tax collection

In 2010-11, the collection of revenue
from State Excise was increased by
17.74 per cent over the previous year
due to increase in number of Bonded
Warehouses, enforcement activities
and revision of licence fee.

Internal audit not conducted

Audit of State Excise Department has
not been conducted by the Examiner
of Local Accounts over the past few
years. This resultantly had its impact

Chapter III: State Excise

CHAPTER 11: STATE EXCISE

3.1 Tax administration

The Principal Secretary, Excise, Registration, Taxation and Stamps (ERTS)
Department is the head of the Excise Department at the Government level. At
the Department level, the Commissioner of Excise (CE) monitors the
functioning of the Department. The implementing authority at the district
level is the Superintendent of Excise (SE), who is responsible for the
collection of all excise duties and fees as also for the proper functioning of the
bonded warehouses and distilleries. The Assam Excise Act and Rules, the
Assam Distillery Rules and the Assam Bonded Warehouse Rules (adopted by
Meghalaya) regulate all excise related activities including revenue collection
in the State. The Excise Department is one of the highest revenue earning
departments in the State, after Taxation and Mining & Geology departments.

However during scrutiny of the records of the ACE, Shillong and

We are of the view that since there is no provision in the Rules ibid which
exempt defence and paramilitary forces from payment of the IPF or formal
executive instructions to this effect, non-levy of the same was erroneous
besides resulting in a recurring revenue loss to the public purse. We further
ascertained that in Assam, the IPF was being levied on IMFL/beer/wine
imported into that State by defence and paramilitary forces.

We recommend that the Department/Government take a considered decision
on whether to exempt or levy the IPF on IMFL/beer/wine imported into the
State by defence and paramilitary forces. If the decision is to exempt the
imports, the Rules ibid will then need to be amended accordingly.

We reported the case to the ERTS Department in June 2011; reply was
awaited (September 2011).

Chapter IV ~ Motor Vehicles Receipts

4.1 7 administration

The Secretary, Transport Department is the head of the Department at the
Government level. At the Department level, the Commissioner of Transport
(CT) is the administrative in-charge and is responsible for overseeing the
functioning of various wings of the Department. The Deputy Commissioner
of Transport, who is also the ex-officio secretary, State Transport Authority
(STA), assists him. At the district level, the District Transport Officer (DTO),
who is also the secretary, Regional Transport Authority (RTA) is responsible
for collection of receipts under the provisions of the various acts and rules.
The administration of the Department and collection of receipts are regulated
by the Motor Vehicles (MV) Act, 1988 and the Assam Motor Vehicles
Taxation (AMVT) Act, 1936 (as adopted by the Government of Meghalaya)
and various rules made thereunder. In addition, the Department has an
Enforcement Branch (EB) headed by a DTO, for enforcement of the rules in
force.

Highlights

The Performance Audit on ‘Computerisation in the Motor Vehicle
Department’ revealed the following:

> We noticed that the Transport Department (TD) had not entered into
any annual maintenance contract (AMC) for maintenance and repairing of
hardware. Further, 67 per cent of the servers, 17 per cent of the slim PCs, 55
per cent of the printers, and 75 per cent of UPS with the Department were not
functioning as on 30 August 2011.

(Paragraph 4.7.8.1)

> We noticed that out of the seven DTOs, four DTOs had not completed
computerisation of backlog records of 12,150 registered vehicles. The backlog
of records of 2.07 lakh driving licenses was not cleared in all the seven DTOs.
Thus the State and National Registers were incomplete to that extent.

(Paragraph 4.7.8.2, 4.7.10.4)

> We noticed that there was no fire detection equipment or fire

extinguishers to fight any contingencies, in any of the three DTOs visited and

the Office of the STA housing the computer for the National Permit System.
(Paragraph 4.7.8.3)

> We noticed that in three DTOs the server were switched on and off on
a daily basis, while in one DTO, the server was switched off at varying
intervals and on holidays and weekends. The department has not laid down
any guidelines laying down the necessity of switching on/off the servers. We
also noticed that log files were deleted without conducting any analysis
leading to loss of audit trail.

(Paragraph 4.7.8.9)

> We noticed that surcharge was not realised from 399 vehicles out of
19,719 private vehicles registered in East Khasi Hill Division. The rates of
surcharge could be altered manually as such manual intervention cannot be
ruled out due to this.

(Paragraph 4.7.9.1)

> The data available in the system indicated that three vehicles shared
same registration number, 1,308 vehicles shared 654 registration numbers,
1,277 vehicles shared the same set of engine numbers and 44 vehicles shared
22 chassis numbers. Out of these, 112 vehicles were registered after
implementation of Vahan.

(Paragraph 4.7.9.3 and 4.7.9.8)

Chapter IV — Motor Vehicles Receipts

4.7.1 Introduction

To achieve faster and better services, transparency and better monitoring of
revenues generated from implementation of the Motor Vehicle Act and Rules,
the Government of India provided standardised softwares ‘Vahan’ and
‘Sarathi’, developed by National Informatics Centre (NIC) to the Transport
Department (TD), Government of Meghalaya (GOM). The TD was also
provided with technical assistance from the NIC, free of charge, for
customisation and backend integration.

Consequent upon a new National Permit (NP) composite fee regime becoming
effective from 07 May 2010 for national goods carrier vehicles, an electronic
system of grant of NP was developed by Ministry of Road Transport and
Highways (MoRTH) in consultation with NIC, New Delhi. Access to this
software was made available to the State though a National Portal.

4.7.2__ Organisational setup

The Principal Secretary to the Government of Meghalaya, Transport
Department is the administrative head of the TD. He is assisted by the
Commissioner of Transport (CT) who heads the Commissionerate of
Transport. The CT is also the ex-officio Secretary of the State Transport
Authority (STA) and is assisted by a Assistant Commissioner of Transport.
An Enforcement Wing is also attached to the Directorate and is headed by a
District Transport Officer (DTO). At the district level, the office is headed by
a DTO who is also the ex-officio Secretary, Regional Transport Authority
(RTA). There are seven 110४ in the State. The function of the CT is to
oversee the functioning of the various wings of the TD and implementation of
the Acts and Rules governing the assessment, levy and collection of motor
vehicle taxes, fees and fines while the DTO Enforcement Wing is responsible
for enforcing the provision of the Acts and Rules. The primary function of the
STA is to grant inter-state permits and that of RTA is to grant regional road
permits to transport vehicles.

4.7.3 Objectives of the computerisation

At the all India level, the main objectives were:

> to develop a national database by developing and providing a uniform
software which could be used by all the States for computerisation of
Transport Offices responsible for issuing vehicle registration
certificates and driving licences;

> to have common specification for the software, to ensure that
documents issued by any State are readable and interoperable
throughout the country; and,

> to have interconnectivity amongst DTOs in the State and a National
Register of Motor Vehicles.

DTOs East Khasi Hills, West Khasi Hills, Jaintia Hills, Ri Bhoi, West Garo Hills, East
Garo Hills and South Garo Hills.

75


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2011-Revenue Receipts

At the State level, the objectives were:

> to bring about transparency in the system;
> to ease and speed up all work related to vehicle registrations and
driving licences;

> to connect the entire State using the networking facility;

> to assist in monitoring work related to vehicle registrations and driving
licences; and

> to create State Register of vehicles registered and driving licenses

issued.

The audit objectives were to ascertain whether:

> the objectives of computerisation through the NIC developed
Information Technology (IT) applications of ‘Vahan’, ‘Sarathi’ and
‘National Permit System’ (NPS) were achieved;

> the phase wise implementation of the two application (‘Vahan’ and
“Sarathi’) were achieved as per time frames fixed;

> computerised systems implemented were complete (module-wise) and
data captured by the DTO were correct and complete;

> connectivity was established between the DTOs in the State for

creation of State Registers of vehicles and licences and National
Registers and Central Servers were put in place towards achievement
of above stated objectives;

> reliable controls were in place to ensure data security and audit trail
besides back up of data for loss of data/crash of systems; and,
> internal control mechanism was in place at the State level to monitor

the implementation of two IT applications.
7.5 Audit criteria

The Performance Audit was carried out based on the following criteria:

Motor Vehicles Act, 1988 (MV Act);

Central Motor Vehicles Rules, 1989;

Assam Motor Vehicles Taxation Act, 1936 (as adopted and amended
by Meghalaya)

Assam Motor Vehicles Taxation Rules, 1936 (as adopted and amended
by Meghalaya)

Meghalaya Motor Vehicle Taxation (Amendment) Act 1973,
Notification issued by the Government of India;

Notification issued by the State Government

Circulars issued from time to time

Motor Vehicle (New High Security Registration Plates) Order, 2001
Vahan and Sarathi software as developed by NIC.
